Instruments

G Arp

A tempo-locked MIDI arpeggiator with a 16-step pattern, accents, ties and a human feel.

Overview

G Arp turns the chords you hold into arpeggios. It sits in front of any instrument on the track and sends out the notes one at a time (or as repeated chords), in the order and rhythm you choose.

Each step of the 16-step pattern can be on, off (a rest), accented or tied to the step before, and has its own level. FEEL adds a human touch: a little timing looseness, velocity variation and a chance for steps to be skipped.

When the project plays, G Arp locks to the tempo and the timeline. When it is stopped, it runs free at the project tempo from the first key you press. Anything that isn't a note - controllers, pitch bend and so on - passes straight through.

Reach for it for

  • Classic synth arpeggios from held chords
  • Rhythmic gated chords (CHORD mode with a pattern)
  • Sequenced basslines and plucks
  • Latching a pattern while you play something else
  • Adding loose, human movement to programmed parts

Setting it up

  1. Insert G Arp in the track's FX chain, above (before) the instrument plugin. It takes MIDI in and sends MIDI on to the instrument; it does not touch audio.
  2. Arm the track and turn on record monitoring (or play MIDI items on the track). The MIDI light blinks green when notes arrive.
  3. Hold a chord: the display shows the step and the notes in the arpeggio. Press play to lock it to the project tempo; when stopped it runs at the project tempo from your first key.
  4. Switch ARP ON off to pass your notes straight through to the instrument.

Controls

Left-hand strip

ARP ON
Turns the arpeggiator on (red light) or off. Off, your notes pass straight through; switching it off stops whatever it was playing.
LATCH
On: the arpeggio keeps playing after you let go of the keys. Release all keys and play a new chord to replace it. Turning LATCH off keeps only the keys you are still holding.
MIDI
Blinks green when a note arrives.
PATTERNS
Opens a menu of step patterns. Choosing one sets the step levels, accents and ties; the knobs and switches stay as they are (except Reset all to defaults).
S / M / L, ?
Panel size, and the pop-up with the design credit, the GFX website and the address of this manual (click anywhere to close it).

TIME

RATE
The length of each step: 1/4, 1/8, 1/8T, 1/16, 1/16T or 1/32 (T = triplet).
SWING
0 to 75%. Lengthens the first step of each pair and shortens the second, for a shuffle.
GATE
How long each note is held, as a percentage of its step, from 10% (short and staccato) to 200%. Above 100% each note overlaps the next, for a legato sound.

RANGE

OCTAVES
1 to 4. The arpeggio repeats the held notes this many octaves upwards.
STEPS
1 to 16: the length of the pattern. Steps beyond it are dimmed and skipped.

FEEL

TIMING
Humanised timing, 0 to 30 ms. Notes land a little late by varying amounts, with a slowly wandering feel rather than pure randomness.
VELOCITY
Humanised velocity, 0 to 40. Each note's velocity varies up or down by up to this amount.
CHANCE
0 to 100%: the chance that each step plays. Below 100% some steps are skipped at random.

NOTES

ACCENT
0 to 60: how much velocity an accented step adds.
LEVEL
1 to 127: the velocity used when the velocity source is FIXED.

Display and modes

Display
The top line shows the mode, rate and octaves. The bottom line shows the current step and the notes in the arpeggio, PLAY A CHORD when nothing is held, or ARP OFF - THRU.
UP / DOWN
The held notes from lowest to highest, or highest to lowest, through all the octaves.
UP-DN / DN-UP
Up then down, or down then up, without repeating the top and bottom notes.
PLAYED
The notes in the order you played them.
RANDOM
A random note from the set on every step.
CHORD
The whole chord on every step; with OCTAVES above 1 each step moves up an octave in turn.
VEL: PLAYED / FIXED / PATTERN
Click to choose where the velocity comes from: the velocity you played each key with, the LEVEL knob, or each step's level in the pattern display.

The step pattern

Step display
The bars above the buttons show each step's level. Click and drag in the display to draw the levels; drag a bar right to the bottom to make it a rest. The level sets the velocity in PATTERN mode. Shift+click a bar to accent it (a red cap), right-click to tie it (TIE). The step playing now is lit.
Step buttons 1 - 16
Click to turn a step on or off (it remembers its level). Shift+click to accent it - its number shows a > sign. Right-click to tie it to the step before: the previous note is held on through this step instead of a new one playing.

Patterns

Straight
Every step on at full level.
Offbeat accents
Every step on, with accents on steps 3, 7, 11 and 15.
Gallop
Rests on steps 2, 6, 10 and 14: a galloping rhythm.
Trance gate
A syncopated on / off gate pattern across the 16 steps.
Rise
Every step on, with levels rising from 35% to full across the bar.
Random
Random levels, with about a quarter of the steps resting and a few random accents. Each choice gives a new pattern.
Tied pairs
Rests on steps 4, 8, 12 and 16, with steps 2, 6, 10 and 14 tied to the step before.
Reset all to defaults
Every step on, and all the knobs and switches back to their starting values.

Tips

  • The step levels only change the velocity when VEL is set to PATTERN; the pattern's rests, accents and ties work in every velocity mode.
  • Try CHORD mode with a short GATE and the Trance gate pattern for rhythmic, gated chords.
  • Set STEPS to an odd length (say 5 or 7) against a 1/16 RATE for patterns that shift against the bar.
  • Your pattern is saved with the project.

File: gfx-g-arpeggiator.jsfx

Instruments

G Bass 1X

A monophonic analogue-style bass synth with three filters, accent and glide.

Overview

G Bass 1X is a one-note-at-a-time bass synthesizer. Its oscillator section has a saw or pulse wave with pulse-width control, a sub oscillator one or two octaves down, a detuned second oscillator, a unison spread for thick, detuned Reese-style sounds, and noise.

Three filters give three characters: LADDER is warm and round (the bass drops a little as the resonance rises), ACID is sharper and keeps its bass, for squelchy acid lines, and 12 dB is a clean, gentle state-variable filter. Hard-played notes trigger the ACCENT, which opens the filter and hits harder.

GLIDE slides between notes, either always or only when notes overlap. The last note you play always wins. A slow analogue drift moves the pitch, the second oscillator, the pulse width and the cutoff, and every new note gets a slightly different tuning.

Reach for it for

  • Acid lines with accent and slides
  • Fat, round analogue-style basses
  • Detuned, growling Reese basses (UNISON)
  • Clean sub basses (Sine Sub, SUB+)
  • Plucks, zaps and wobbles

Playing it

  1. Insert G Bass 1X on a track (it has no audio input; it makes its own sound).
  2. Arm the track for recording with a MIDI input selected and monitoring on, or draw notes in a MIDI item on the track.
  3. Play: it responds on every MIDI channel, plays one note at a time (the last note held wins) and follows pitch bend over +/-2 semitones. The MIDI light flashes on each note.
  4. Pick a sound by clicking the display for the patch list, or with PATCH - and PATCH +.

Controls

Display and patches

Display
Shows the patch number and name; a * after the number means you have changed it. The second line shows the filter type and glide mode, or the value of the knob you are turning. Click it for the full patch list.
PATCH - / PATCH +
Step to the previous or next patch. A patch sets the oscillators, LFO, filter, envelopes and performance controls; DRIFT, DRIVE, VOLUME, SUB+ and the IN / OUT strip are left as they are.
MIDI
Flashes when a note arrives.

OSCILLATORS

SAW / PULSE
The main oscillator's wave. SAW is bright and buzzy; PULSE is hollower and can be shaped with PW.
PW
Pulse width, 5 to 95% (50% is a square wave). Only with PULSE.
SUB -1 / SUB -2
The sub oscillator's level, 0 to 100%. Click the label to switch it between one and two octaves below the note.
OSC2 -1 / OSC2 0 / OSC2 +1
The level of a second saw oscillator, 0 to 100%. Click the label to set its octave: one down, the same, or one up.
DETUNE
Detunes the second oscillator, 0 to 50 cents. A few cents thickens; more gives a beating, chorused sound.
UNISON
Adds two more saws, detuned either side of the note (up to 30 cents), for a wide, thick, Reese-style sound.
NOISE
Adds white noise, for breath and grit.

LFO

RATE (SINE / TRI / SAW / SQUARE / S&H)
The LFO speed, 0.05 to 20 Hz. The label shows the shape: click it to step through sine, triangle, saw, square and sample-and-hold (random steps).
PITCH
How much the LFO moves the pitch, 0 to 100 cents. Small amounts give vibrato.
CUTOFF
How much the LFO sweeps the filter, up to three octaves - for wobbles.
PWM
How much the LFO moves the pulse width (PULSE wave only).

FILTER

LADDER / ACID / 12 dB
The filter type. LADDER: warm 24 dB per octave. ACID: sharp 24 dB per octave that keeps its bass at high resonance. 12 dB: a clean, gentler state-variable filter.
CUTOFF
The filter frequency, 20 Hz to 18 kHz.
RES
Resonance, 0 to 100%. High settings give the squelch; the ladder filters can self-oscillate near the top.
ENV
How far the filter envelope opens the filter, up to seven octaves.
KEY
Key tracking: how much the cutoff follows the notes you play, so higher notes stay as bright as lower ones (100% = the cutoff moves an octave for each octave played).

FILTER ENVELOPE

ATTACK
0.5 ms to 3 s.
DECAY
5 ms to 5 s.
SUSTAIN
0 to 100%.
RELEASE
5 ms to 5 s.

AMP ENVELOPE

ATTACK
0.5 ms to 3 s.
DECAY
5 ms to 5 s.
SUSTAIN
0 to 100%. Set it to 0 for plucked sounds that die away while you hold the key.
RELEASE
5 ms to 5 s.

When you play a new note while the previous one is still held, the envelopes carry on rather than restarting, as on the classic mono synths.

PERFORMANCE

GLIDE ALL / GLIDE LEG
The glide time, 0 to 1000 ms. Click the label to switch the mode: ALL glides into every note; LEG glides only between overlapping (legato) notes, so you choose the slides by how you play.
ACCENT
How much hard-played notes (above half velocity) open the filter further and hit harder.
VELOCITY
How much the playing strength changes the level, 0 to 100%.

MASTER

DRIFT
The analogue drift, 0 to 100% (25% by default). See Drift below.
DRIVE
Gentle saturation after the filter, 0 to 100% (15% by default).
VOLUME
The synth's level, -24 to +6 dB.

Drift

In G Bass 1X, drift moves the pitch (up to +/-20 cents at 100%), the second oscillator's tuning, the pulse width and the filter cutoff, slowly and randomly. Every new note also gets a small random tuning offset (up to +/-6 cents at 100%). 0% switches it all off.

IN / OUT

SAT
Band saturation on the way out: only the lows and highs are saturated (the mids stay clean), with a soft curve that loosens as the level rises.
OFF / CLIP / LIMIT (OUT)
The final stage: nothing, a soft clipper at -0.3 dBFS (no latency, the default), or a lookahead limiter holding the peaks at -0.3 dBFS (3 ms latency, compensated by REAPER). The CLIP key flashes red while it is clipping.

SUB+

ON
Adds a pure sine an octave below the note, plus soft harmonics an octave above it. Both follow the amp envelope. Useful for weight on small speakers and for basses that need to be heard as well as felt.
SUB
The level of the sine an octave below (50% by default).
UPPER
The level of the harmonics an octave above (30% by default).

Size and help

S / M / L
Panel size: small, medium or large.
?
Opens a pop-up with the design credit, the GFX website address and the address of this manual. Click anywhere to close it.

Presets

Mono Acid
Saw into the ACID filter at 300 Hz with high resonance, a short filter decay, strong accent and legato glide - the squelchy acid line.
Mono Sub
A 40% pulse with a strong sub oscillator through a gently resonant ACID filter.
Mono PWM
Pulse with a slow triangle LFO on the pulse width, some sub and legato glide.
Ladder Fat
Saw plus a second saw detuned 8 cents and some sub, through the warm LADDER filter.
Ladder Pluck
Detuned saws through the LADDER filter with a strong envelope and the amp sustain at 0, for a pluck.
Ladder Glide
Saws with the second oscillator an octave up, a brighter LADDER filter, 90 ms legato glide and a touch of vibrato.
Wide Reese
Saws detuned 22 cents with UNISON at 60% through the clean 12 dB filter, a slow LFO moving the cutoff.
Wide Growl
Detuned saws with unison and a little noise, a resonant LADDER filter swept by a triangle LFO.
Sine Sub
Pulse with a heavy sub through the 12 dB filter at 250 Hz, no resonance: an almost pure sub bass.
Synth Pop
A narrow 30% pulse with sub and a detuned second oscillator through a bright ACID filter.
Zap Bass
Saw through the ACID filter at 200 Hz with the envelope fully up and a 90 ms decay - a zap on every note.
Wobble
Detuned saws with unison through a resonant LADDER filter, a 3 Hz sine LFO sweeping the cutoff.

Tips

  • For acid lines, set GLIDE to LEG and play overlapping notes where you want slides; vary the velocity for accents.
  • If the bass disappears on small speakers, switch SUB+ on and raise UPPER a little.
  • Raise KEY when playing higher lines, so the filter doesn't sound duller the higher you go.
  • Lower VOLUME rather than relying on CLIP if the CLIP key flashes on every note.

File: gfx-g-bass-1x.jsfx

Instruments

G Chip 48

A chiptune sound module with five 8-bit and 16-bit era sound chips, tracker-style arps and built-in drum kits.

Overview

G Chip 48 is a chiptune synth built as a rack sound module. It recreates the sound chips of the 8-bit home computers and early games consoles: the one-bit BEEPER of the 48K home computers, the three-voice AY chip of the 128K machines with its buzzer envelope, the PSG square-wave chip with its white and periodic noise, the four-operator FM chip of the 16-bit consoles, and the PULSE of the 8-bit consoles with its 12.5, 25 and 50% pulses and stepped triangle. The sounds are designed from scratch.

Each chip behaves like the real thing: pitches snap to the chip's own tuning steps, volume snaps to its own level steps, and by default each chip has only as many voices as the original. The envelope, arpeggio, vibrato and pitch sweep all step once a frame (50 or 60 times a second), the way the old music drivers and trackers did it.

The rubber keys along the top pick the chip and the patch; the knobs below shape the sound. Each chip also has its own drum kit on the General MIDI drum notes.

Reach for it for

  • Chiptune and video-game music
  • Retro arpeggio chords, bleepy leads and buzzy basses
  • Lasers, zaps and 8-bit drum kits
  • 16-bit FM basses, brass and electric pianos
  • Adding a lo-fi 8-bit layer to modern synth parts

Playing it

  1. Create a track and add G Chip 48 as its effect: it is an instrument, so it has no audio input and produces stereo output.
  2. Arm the track for recording with your MIDI keyboard as the input and monitoring on, or draw notes in a MIDI item on the track.
  3. Pick a chip with keys 1 - 5, or click the screen for the full patch list. The MIDI IN light flashes as notes arrive.
  4. By default it listens on all MIDI channels (OMNI). Click CH to choose a single channel if you drive several instruments from one source.

MIDI: pitch bend covers +-2 semitones; the mod wheel (CC1) adds vibrato; the sustain pedal (CC64) holds notes; All Notes Off (CC123) and All Sound Off (CC120) stop everything.

Controls

Top left

CH (MIDI)
The MIDI channel it listens to: OMNI (all channels) or 1 - 16. Click to step up; Shift-click to step down.
TV (50 Hz / 60 Hz)
The frame rate: PAL (50 Hz) or NTSC (60 Hz). Everything that steps per frame - envelope, arp, vibrato, sweep and glide - runs at this rate, so NTSC is slightly faster.
MIDI IN
Lights when a note arrives.
S / M / L
Panel size: small, medium or large. The panel also scales to fit the plugin window.
?
Opens a pop-up with the design credit, the GFX website address and the address of this manual. Click anywhere to close it.

The screen

Shows the patch number and name, the chip, how many voices are playing out of how many are available, the frame rate, and ARP, HELD ARP or DRUMS when they are on. The border takes the chip's colour and runs loading stripes while notes play. Click the screen for the full patch list, grouped by chip.

The number keys

1 - 5 (chip)
1 BEEPER, 2 AY, 3 PSG, 4 FM, 5 PULSE. Pressing a key loads that chip's first patch. The lit key shows the current chip.
6 PATCH < PREV
The previous patch (wrapping round).
7 PATCH NEXT >
The next patch (wrapping round).
8 DRUMS
Switches the chip's drum kit on or off (see Drums below).
9 VOICES
CHIP keeps each chip's own voice count: BEEPER 1, AY and PSG 3, FM 6, PULSE 2 (1 when the mixer is on TRI). 6 lifts every chip to six voices. When all voices are busy, the oldest note is taken over.
0 PANIC
Stops every sounding note at once.

TONE / NOISE

DUTY
The pulse width, 5 to 50%. On the BEEPER the width also falls with the volume, which is how the one-bit speaker played loud and soft. On the PULSE it snaps to the chip's three widths: 12.5, 25 or 50%. The other chips always play a 50% square.
MIXER
TONE: the square or pulse. T+N: tone and noise combined, for a gritty, steamy sound. NOISE: noise only. TRI: a stepped 4-bit triangle, as on the PULSE.
NOISE
The noise pitch, 0 to 100 (70 by default). Higher is brighter and hissier.
TYPE
WHITE or PERIODIC noise, on the PSG and PULSE. On the PSG, periodic noise is a pitched buzz that follows the note; on the PULSE it is the chip's short-loop metallic noise.

BUZZ

AY only: the AY's envelope generator run at audio rate - its famous buzzer bass and lead sounds.

SHAPE
OFF, SAW, TRI or SYNC. SAW and TRI play the envelope as a waveform; SYNC gates it with the tone for a harder, synced sound.
TUNE
The buzz tuned an octave down, in unison or an octave up from the note. Its period also snaps to the chip's own steps.

ENVELOPE

Stepped once a frame, as on the chips.

ATTACK
0 to 2000 ms. 0 starts the note at full level.
DECAY
0 to 5000 ms (400 ms by default): the fall from full level to the sustain level.
SUSTAIN
0 to 15, the chips' own volume steps (12 by default). At 0 the note dies away after the decay, for plucks and bells.
RELEASE
0 to 5000 ms (120 ms by default): the fade after you let go.

ARP

CHORD
OFF, MAJ, MIN, OCT, 5TH, MAJ7 or MIN7: each note becomes a fast tracker-style arpeggio of that chord on a single voice. HELD steps through every note you hold, in the order you played them, on one voice.
SPEED
How many frames each arp step lasts, 1 to 8 (1 is the fastest).

VIBRATO

DEPTH
0 to 100 cents.
DELAY
How long after the note starts the vibrato comes in, 0 to 1000 ms (250 ms by default).
RATE
1 to 12 Hz (6 Hz by default).

PITCH

GLIDE
Slides from the last note to the new one, 0 to 1000 ms. 0 is off.
SWEEP
Each note starts this far away from its pitch, -48 to +48 semitones, and sweeps back to it: lasers, zaps and drum-like drops.
TIME
How long the sweep takes, 10 to 2000 ms (150 ms by default).

FM

FM only, except DETUNE / PHASER. The operator tunings and levels come from the FM patch you load; these knobs shape them.

ALGO
Which of the eight four-operator algorithms is used, 0 to 7: from all four operators in a stack (the most complex tones) to all four as carriers (organ-like).
FEEDBACK
Operator 1 feeding back on itself, 0 to 7. Higher values add buzz and noise.
MOD
The overall modulation level, 0 to 200% (100% by default). More is brighter.
MOD DECAY
Scales how long the modulators take to die away, 10 to 400%. Short gives plucky, percussive tones; long keeps them bright.
DETUNE / PHASER
On FM, detunes the operators slightly against each other, 0 to 100%. On the BEEPER the knob reads PHASER and mixes a second, slightly detuned square, for the one-bit phasing sound.
LADDER
The grit of the early FM chip's converter, 0 to 100% (50% by default): coarser steps and a little step at the zero crossing.

OUTPUT

VELO
How much velocity changes the level, 0 to 100% (30% by default). 0 plays every note at full level.
LO-FI
Blends in the original machine's own speaker or output filter, 0 to 100% (60% by default): the BEEPER's little speaker (280 Hz to 4.2 kHz), the FM console's early output filter (rolled off from 3.4 kHz), and gentler filtering on the others.
STEREO
Spreads the voices across the stereo field, 0 to 100% (40% by default). 0 is mono.
VOLUME
The output level, -24 to +6 dB. Each patch is already level-matched, so they play at about the same loudness.

There is no input or output fold-out on this panel. The output passes through the GFX soft clipper, which catches peaks at -0.3 dBFS.

Drums

With DRUMS on, each chip plays its own kit on the General MIDI drum notes: 35 / 36 kick, 37 - 40 snare, 42 / 44 closed hi-hat, 46 open hi-hat, 49 / 52 / 55 / 57 crash, 51 / 53 / 59 ride. Toms (41 - 50) and every other note play pitched toms. The drums use six voices and ignore the envelope, arp and vibrato.

Presets

The patches set the chip and every sound control (tone, buzz, envelope, arp, vibrato, pitch, FM and drums). MIDI channel, frame rate, voices, velocity, lo-fi, stereo and volume are left as they are.

BEEPER

48K Lead
A plain square lead with light vibrato.
Thin Pulse
A thin 10% pulse lead.
Phaser Lead
The one-bit phaser sound (PHASER at 30%).
Beeper Bass
A short 30% pulse bass with a little glide.
Chord Arp
HELD arp: play a chord and it cycles through the notes.
Laser Zap
A 36-semitone sweep down onto the note with no sustain.
Beeper Drums
The beeper drum kit.
Soft Hum
A soft 25% pulse with a slow attack, long release and gentle vibrato.

AY

AY Lead
A square lead with vibrato.
Buzzer Bass
The saw-shaped buzzer in unison: the classic AY bass.
Buzz Tri Bass
The buzzer with a triangle shape, softer.
Sync Lead
The buzzer synced to the tone, an octave up, with vibrato.
Arp Chords
HELD arp chords.
Soft Bell
A plucked square that dies away, with a little vibrato.
Steam Lead
Tone and noise combined for a breathy lead.
AY Drums
The AY drum kit.

PSG

PSG Lead
A square lead with vibrato.
PSG Bass
A short square bass.
Periodic Bass
Periodic noise played as a pitched, buzzy bass.
Major Arp
A major-chord arp, 2 frames a step.
Minor Arp
A minor-chord arp, 2 frames a step.
PSG Bell
A plucked square that dies away.
Vib Square
A square with deep, immediate vibrato.
PSG Drums
The PSG drum kit.

FM

16-Bit Bass
A punchy FM bass with extra feedback and ladder grit.
FM Brass
Brass with a short attack, vibrato and a little detune.
FM E.Piano
An electric piano with a long decay and no sustain.
FM Lead
A bright lead with strong feedback and vibrato.
FM Strings
Slow attack and release with plenty of detune.
FM Marimba
A short, woody mallet sound.
FM Organ
All four operators as carriers, full sustain.
FM Drums
The FM drum kit.

PULSE

Pulse 12.5
The thinnest of the chip's pulses.
Pulse 25 Lead
The 25% pulse with vibrato.
Square 50
The plain 50% square.
Triangle Bass
The stepped triangle as a bass (one voice).
Echo Arp
A 25% pulse major-chord arp.
Vibrato Lead
A 25% pulse with deep vibrato.
Slide Lead
A square lead with glide between notes.
8-Bit Drums
The PULSE drum kit.

Init

Init
A plain BEEPER square with a basic envelope: a starting point for your own sounds.

Tips

  • For authentic chip chords, use an ARP chord or HELD rather than playing chords with more voices than the chip has.
  • Keep VOICES on CHIP for the real thing: note stealing on a one-voice BEEPER is part of the sound.
  • Lasers and drum drops: set SUSTAIN to 0 and use a large SWEEP with a short TIME.
  • Turn LO-FI down for a cleaner, fuller sound in a modern mix; up for the original small-speaker character.

File: gfx-g-chip.jsfx

Instruments

G Cosmos

An 8-voice analogue pad synthesizer in the spirit of the classic 80s polys, with a detuned layer, chorus and an ambient delay.

Overview

G Cosmos is a polyphonic pad synth in the spirit of the classic 80s polys, with a nod to the layered shimmer of the late-80s digital synths. One oscillator per voice gives saw, pulse (with PWM), a sub-octave and noise; a second, detuned saw (the LAYER) can sit at the same pitch or an octave either side for width and shimmer.

The voices go through a 24 dB filter with its own envelope, a fixed high-pass with four settings, and an amp envelope. The famous bucket-brigade stereo chorus (I, II or both) and an ambient stereo delay with diffused, gently moving repeats finish the sound.

DRIFT makes it behave like real analogue hardware: the tuning and filter wander for the whole instrument and for each voice on its own, and every note lands slightly differently in tune.

Reach for it for

  • Lush, slow-moving pads and drones
  • Warm 80s poly chords and string machines
  • PWM and choir-like textures
  • Filter-sweep pads for builds and intros
  • Ambient beds with long, diffused delay tails

Playing it

G Cosmos is an instrument: it has no audio input and makes its sound from MIDI.

  1. Insert G Cosmos on a track (as the first plug-in, or after a MIDI effect).
  2. Arm the track for recording and set its input to your MIDI keyboard (or draw notes in a MIDI item).
  3. Pick a patch with < and >, or click the patch display for the list.
  4. Play. The MIDI light flashes on each note, and the display shows how many of the 8 voices are sounding.
  5. If you use a multi-channel MIDI setup, set CH in the PERFORMANCE section to the channel you want; OMNI listens to all of them.

G Cosmos responds to pitch bend (+/-2 semitones), the mod wheel (CC1 adds vibrato through the LFO, up to 40 cents), the sustain pedal (CC64) and all-notes-off (CC120 and CC123).

Controls

The header

Patch display
Shows the patch number and name, how many of the 8 voices are playing and the chorus setting. Click it to open the patch list.
< and >
Step to the previous or next patch. A patch sets every sound control and VELOCITY; DRIFT, VOLUME and the MIDI channel are left as they are. Loading a patch stops any notes still sounding.
MIDI
Lights when a note arrives.
S / M / L
Panel size: small, medium or large. The panel also scales to fit the plugin window.
?
Opens a pop-up with the design credit, the GFX website address and the address of this manual. Click anywhere to close it.

LFO

RATE
LFO speed, 0.05 - 20 Hz.
DELAY
How long the LFO takes to fade in, 0 - 5 s. It starts again from the first key of each new phrase (when no other keys are held), so held chords grow vibrato and movement as they sustain.
SHAPE
SINE, TRI, SQUARE or RANDOM (stepped).
PITCH
Vibrato depth, 0 - 100 cents. The mod wheel adds up to 40 cents more.

The LFO also moves the filter (LFO in the VCF section) and the pulse width (PWM in the DCO section).

DCO

SAW
Level of the sawtooth, 0 - 100%.
PULSE
Level of the pulse wave, 0 - 100%.
PW
The pulse width, 5 - 95%. 50% is a square; narrower settings sound thinner and more nasal.
PWM
How much the LFO sweeps the pulse width, for the classic moving, chorused pulse sound.
SUB
Level of a square wave one octave below.
NOISE
Level of white noise, for breath and air.

LAYER

LEVEL
Level of the second, detuned saw, 0 - 100%.
DETUNE
How far the layer is detuned, 0 - 50 cents. Small amounts give a slow beating width; large amounts a thick, wide shimmer.
OCTAVE
The layer's octave: -1, 0 or +1.

HPF

0 / 1 / 2 / 3
The fixed high-pass after the filter. 0 BOOST lifts the bass below about 120 Hz; 1 FLAT leaves it alone; 2 and 3 THIN cut the lows (from about 240 Hz and 720 Hz), so the pad sits above a bass part.

VCF

CUTOFF
The 24 dB low-pass filter's cutoff, 20 Hz - 18 kHz.
RES
Resonance, 0 - 100%. High settings ring and sharpen the sweep.
ENV
How far the FILTER ENV moves the cutoff, -100 to +100%. Negative values sweep it down instead of up.
LFO
How much the LFO moves the cutoff, 0 - 100%.
KEY
Key tracking, 0 - 100%: how much higher notes open the filter. At 100% the cutoff follows the keyboard.

FILTER ENV and AMP ENV

Two separate ADSR envelopes: FILTER ENV moves the cutoff (by the ENV amount), AMP ENV shapes the volume.

ATTACK
Time to rise, 1 ms - 8 s.
DECAY
Time to fall to the sustain level, 5 ms - 10 s.
SUSTAIN
The level held while the key is down, 0 - 100%.
RELEASE
Time to die away after the key is let go, 5 ms - 15 s.

CHORUS

I / II
The bucket-brigade stereo chorus. I is a slow, gentle sweep; II is faster and deeper. Light both for I+II, where the two sweeps run together. With both off the chorus is out.

DELAY

An ambient stereo delay: the repeats bounce between left and right (the right side at three-quarters of the time), are diffused, move gently and get darker as they fade.

TIME
Delay time, 40 - 1500 ms.
FEEDBACK
How many repeats, 0 - 95%.
TONE
How dark the repeats are, 500 Hz - 12 kHz.
MIX
How loud the delay is, 0 - 100%.

PERFORMANCE

VELOCITY
How much playing harder makes notes louder, 0 - 100%. At 0% every note plays at the same level.
DRIFT
See Drift below.
VOLUME
Output level, -24 to +6 dB.
CH
The MIDI channel G Cosmos listens to: OMNI (all) or CH 1 to CH 16.

The output is softly clipped at -0.3 dBFS, so peaks never go over.

Drift

DRIFT
0 - 100% (25% by default). The tuning wanders slowly for the whole instrument (up to 20 cents at 100%) and for each voice on its own (up to 6 cents); the filter cutoff wanders the same way, for the whole instrument and per voice; the layer's detune drifts a little; and every note starts slightly out of tune (up to 6 cents). At 0% everything stays exact.

Presets

Cosmos
The starting sound: saw and sub with a detuned layer, a slow swell, chorus I and an ambient delay.
Warm Poly
Saw and sub with the bass BOOST, a darker filter, a quicker attack and chorus II.
Strings 6
Bright saws with an octave-up layer, a thinner HPF, gentle vibrato and both choruses.
Glass Shimmer
A strong, widely detuned layer an octave up, a bright filter, long release and a long, wet delay.
Dark Drone
Bass BOOST, a low, resonant filter with slow LFO movement, a sub-octave layer, very slow attack and release and a long, dark delay.
PWM Haze
A pulse wave swept by the LFO (PWM) with sub, over chorus I.
Brass Pad
Punchy saws with a strong filter envelope, a fast attack, light vibrato and more velocity.
Choir Air
A narrow pulse with PWM, saw, noise and a layer, resonant and thin, with vibrato and both choruses.
Sweep Pad
A low, resonant filter that slowly opens on each chord (slow filter attack and decay).
Slow Strings
Saws with a strong layer, a very slow attack, vibrato and both choruses.
Nebula
Everything: saw, PWM pulse, sub, noise and a wide octave-up layer, a random LFO on the filter, very long envelopes and a long, heavy delay.
Init
The default settings (the same as Cosmos).

Tips

  • For pads that bloom, set the LFO DELAY to 1 - 2 s and add a little PITCH and filter LFO: each new chord starts still and grows movement.
  • In a busy mix, use HPF 2 or 3 so the pad stays out of the way of the bass.
  • A negative ENV with a high CUTOFF gives pads that darken as they sustain.
  • Chorus I+II with a little layer DETUNE is the widest setting; try it on a single held note before adding more voices.
  • Use VELOCITY at 0% for even sequenced pads, and higher for played parts.

File: gfx-g-cosmos.jsfx

Instruments

G Ensemble

A divide-down string machine with a three-phase ensemble.

Overview

G Ensemble is a string machine in the spirit of the classic 70s string synths. Like the originals, it uses twelve master oscillators and divides them down for every octave, so every key can sound at once, nothing is ever cut off, and all the notes of the same name are locked in phase.

Four registers are mixed together: VIOLIN (a bright 8'), VIOLA (a mellow 8'), CELLO (16') and BASS (16' plus 32', on the lower keys only). Each key has its own simple envelope: ATTACK fades it in, RELEASE lets it ring on after you let go.

The magic is the ENSEMBLE: three delay lines swept by a slow and a fast wobble, 120 degrees apart. It turns the plain, buzzy oscillators into the lush, shimmering string sound.

Reach for it for

  • Lush 70s and 80s string pads
  • Slow swells and evolving backgrounds
  • Disco string stabs
  • Dark cello and bass drones
  • Wide, shimmering layers under other keyboards

Playing it

G Ensemble is an instrument: it has no audio input and makes its sound from MIDI.

  1. Insert G Ensemble on a track.
  2. Arm the track for recording and set its input to your MIDI keyboard (or draw notes in a MIDI item).
  3. Play. The MIDI IN light glows while notes are sounding, and the display shows how many keys are sounding.
  4. If you use a multi-channel MIDI setup, set CHANNEL in the MIDI section; OMNI listens to all channels.

There is no limit on how many keys sound at once. It responds to all-notes-off (CC120 and CC123); incoming MIDI is passed on down the chain.

Controls

The header

PRESETS, arrows and display
PRESETS (or a click on the display) opens the list of factory presets; the ◄ / ► keys step through them. The display shows the preset number and name. A preset sets the STRINGS, ENSEMBLE and TONE controls; TUNE, the MIDI channel, DRIFT, OUTPUT, SAT, the output stage and the stereo controls are left as they are. The last entry resets everything to the defaults.
S / M / L
Panel size: small, medium or large. The panel also scales to fit the plugin window.
?
Opens a pop-up with the design credit, the GFX website address and the address of this manual. Click anywhere to close it.
PANIC
Stops every key at once.

The display

Shows the three ensemble wobbles moving, ENSEMBLE THREE-PHASE (or ENSEMBLE OFF), and KEYS: the number of keys sounding.

STRINGS

VIOLIN
The level of the bright 8' register, 0 - 100%.
VIOLA
The level of the mellow 8' register, 0 - 100%: softer and more hollow than the violin.
CELLO
The level of the 16' register (an octave down), 0 - 100%.
BASS
The level of the 16' and 32' bass register, 0 - 100%. It plays only on the keys below C3 (MIDI note 48).
ATTACK
How long each key takes to fade in, 5 - 2000 ms. Short for stabs, long for swells.
RELEASE
How long each key rings on after you let go, 50 - 5000 ms.
VELOCITY
How much your playing dynamics matter, 0 - 100%. At 0 (default) every note plays at the same level, like an organ, as on the originals.

ENSEMBLE

ON
Switches the ensemble on or off. Off gives the dry, buzzy oscillators.
DEPTH
How far the three delay lines are swept, 0 - 100%. More depth gives more shimmer and detune.
SPEED
The speed of both wobbles, 25 - 200% of the standard rate (100%).
WIDTH
The stereo width of the ensemble, 0 - 100%. At 0 it is mono.

VOICE

TONE
A low-pass tone control on the whole sound, 0 - 100%. Lower is darker.
TUNE
Fine tuning, -50 to +50 cents.

DRIFT

DRIFT
A slow random wander, 0 - 100% (25% by default). It moves the pitch of the whole instrument (up to +/-20 cents at 100%), the TONE, and the speed of the ensemble. The keys stay locked together, as on a divide-down machine.

MIDI

CHANNEL
The MIDI channel G Ensemble listens to: OMNI (all) or 1 - 16.
MIDI IN
Lights when a note arrives and while keys are sounding. The number underneath counts the keys sounding.

MASTER

SAT
Band saturation on the way out, 0 - 100%: only the lows and highs are saturated (the mids stay clean), with a soft curve that loosens as the level rises.
OFF / CLIP / LIMIT
The final stage: nothing, a soft clipper that holds the peaks at -0.3 dBFS (no latency), or a lookahead limiter that holds them at -0.3 dBFS (3 ms latency, compensated by REAPER). The CLIP key flashes red while the clipper is working.
MONO
Makes everything below the set frequency mono, 20 - 500 Hz (fully left = OFF). Use it to keep the low end centred.
WIDTH
Widens the stereo image above the MONO frequency, from +0% up to +100% (twice the side signal).
OUTPUT
The output level, -24 to +12 dB.
OUT meters
Output level, left and right. Click a meter to clear its peak hold.

Presets

Classic Strings
The default: all four registers, a 150 ms attack, a 0.9 s release and the ensemble on.
Violins Only
Just the violin register, a quick attack, a deep, slightly faster and fully wide ensemble, brighter tone.
Dark Cellos
Mostly viola, cello and bass with little violin, a slow attack, a long release and a darker tone.
Space Pad
All registers with a slow 0.9 s attack, a 4 s release and a deep, slow, fully wide ensemble.
Disco Stabs
Violin and viola with a fast attack and short release, and some velocity response for accents.
Full Orchestra-ish
All four registers with plenty of bass and cello, a medium attack and a long release.
Dry (no ensemble)
The default registers with the ensemble off.
Slow Swell
Violin, viola and a little cello with a 1.2 s attack, a 5 s release and the deepest, slowest ensemble.
Reset all to defaults
Every control back to its starting value, including TUNE, the MIDI channel, DRIFT, OUTPUT, SAT, the output stage and the stereo controls.

Tips

  • Hold a chord and switch the ensemble ON and off to hear what it does - most of the sound is in the ensemble.
  • Keep BASS for the lowest part of your part; it only plays below C3, so it adds weight without muddying the chords.
  • For a pad that blooms, set ATTACK to 500 ms or more and RELEASE to 2 s or more, and overlap the chords.
  • If the strings fight with other parts, lower TONE rather than turning them down.

File: gfx-g-ensemble.jsfx

Instruments

G Glow

An 8-voice additive synthesizer in the spirit of the first micro-computer digital synths of 1980-81.

Overview

G Glow builds every sound from harmonic partials. You draw the level of each partial on a green phosphor screen, and the synth turns that drawing into a waveform. It is modelled on the early home-computer digital synths, where a pair of oscillator cards sat inside the computer and you drew the sound on the monitor.

Each voice has two paths, A and B. A path is an additive oscillator (16, 32 or 64 partials) plus a noise generator, with its own envelope, level and pan. The two oscillators can modulate each other (X-MOD), two LFOs add movement, and a stereo dual delay finishes the sound.

Each oscillator can run MODERN - clean and band-limited, with no aliasing - or VINTAGE, which recreates the old card: an 8-bit, 256-byte wavetable read with no smoothing, an 8-bit volume stage that makes the tails step down, and a 31.25 kHz output clock. VINTAGE aliases at the top on purpose; that grit is the sound.

Reach for it for

  • Glassy, bell-like and chime sounds built partial by partial
  • Lo-fi 8-bit basses, leads and arcade percussion (VINTAGE with LFSR noise)
  • Organ, string, flute and choir-like tones from drawn spectra
  • Slow-moving pads and drones with stereo LFO panning and the dual delay
  • Quick sound design: RANDOM makes a new, usable partial set in one click

Playing it

G Glow is an instrument: it has no audio input and makes its sound from MIDI.

  1. Insert G Glow on a track (as the first plug-in, or after a MIDI effect).
  2. Arm the track for recording and set its input to your MIDI keyboard (or draw notes in a MIDI item).
  3. Play. The MIDI light in the header flashes on each note, and the patch display shows how many of the 8 voices are sounding.
  4. If you use a multi-channel MIDI setup, set CHANNEL in the MASTER section to the channel you want; OMNI listens to all of them.

G Glow responds to pitch bend (range set by BEND), the mod wheel (CC1 adds to LFO 1's amount, up to 100%), the sustain pedal (CC64) and all-notes-off (CC120 and CC123). Incoming MIDI is passed on down the chain.

Controls

The header

< and >
Step to the previous or next factory patch.
Patch display
Shows the patch number and name, the voice mode, how many of the 8 voices are playing and SUS while the sustain pedal is down. Click it to open the patch list.
PRESETS
Also opens the patch list. A patch sets every sound control and both partial sets; the MIDI channel, DRIFT, VOLUME, the STEREO controls and the output stage are left as they are.
MIDI
Lights when a note arrives.
S / M / L
Panel size: small, medium or large. The panel also scales to fit the plugin window.
?
Opens a pop-up with the design credit, the GFX website address and the address of this manual. Click anywhere to close it.
PANIC
Stops every sounding note at once. Changing the voice mode does the same.

Oscillator A and Oscillator B

Each oscillator has a screen showing its partials as bars, from the fundamental (1) on the left upwards. The small window at the top right of the screen shows the single wave cycle the partials make.

The screen
Left-drag to draw partial levels; drag across the screen to draw a whole curve in one go. Right-drag to erase. The partial sets are saved with the project.
MODERN / VINTAGE
MODERN: a clean oscillator that leaves out any partials that would go past the top of the audio range, so it never aliases. VINTAGE: the old 8-bit card - a 256-byte wavetable with no smoothing, pitch steps from the old 16-bit counter, an 8-bit volume stage after the envelope (stepped tails) and a 31.25 kHz output clock. It aliases at the top, and the screen shows levels in 16 steps, like the old monitor.
16 / 32 / 64
How many partials the oscillator uses. Fewer partials give a softer, simpler sound and wider bars to draw on.
RANDOM
Makes a new partial set that is still musical: a falling harmonic series with a little variation, odd partials only, random spikes over a fundamental, one to three formant bumps, or a saw with every few partials missing.
SHAPES
A menu of starting points: Sine, Saw, Square, Triangle, Odd emphasis, Soft decay, Formant, Organ (drawbar-style), Even emphasis, Bright tine, and Clear (all partials off).

Path A and Path B

TRANSPOSE
The oscillator's pitch in semitones, -36 to +36.
FINE
Fine tuning, -100 to +100 cents. Opposite settings on A and B give a detuned, chorused width.
PITCH ENV
How far the path's own envelope bends its oscillator's pitch, -48 to +48 semitones at the top of the envelope. Use it with a short decay for thumps, drops and zaps.
LEVEL
The path's volume, 0 - 100%. Path A starts at 80%, Path B at 0%, so a fresh patch only plays A.
PAN
Where the path sits in the stereo field, from L 100 to R 100.
NOISE
Blends from the oscillator to the noise generator: 0% is all oscillator, 100% all noise.
TONE
The brightness of the noise, from about 200 Hz up past the top of the audio range. With WHITE it is a low-pass filter; with LFSR it is the clock rate of the noise.
WHITE / LFSR
WHITE: smooth hiss. LFSR: a 15-bit shift-register noise, the gritty, metallic noise of 80s digital chips - good for snares, hats and arcade effects.

Envelope A and Envelope B

Each path has its own ADSR envelope, which shapes its level and its PITCH ENV. The small screen to the right sketches the envelope's shape.

ATTACK
Time to rise to full level, 0.5 ms to 10 s.
DECAY
Time to fall to the sustain level, 1 ms to 20 s.
SUSTAIN
The level held while the key is down, 0 - 100%.
RELEASE
Time to die away after the key is let go, 1 ms to 20 s.
A CURVE / D CURVE / R CURVE
The shape of each stage, -100 to +100. 0 is a straight line; positive values are exponential and snappy; negative values start slowly. D and R start at +50.

Voice

POLY / MONO / RETRIG
POLY: up to 8 notes at once. MONO: one note, legato - when you play a new note while holding another, the pitch glides and the envelopes carry on. RETRIG: one note, and every note restarts the envelopes.
GLIDE
How long the pitch takes to slide to a new note, 0 - 2000 ms. In POLY each voice glides from the last note it played.
BEND
Pitch-bend range, 0 - 24 semitones.
VELOCITY
How much playing harder makes the sound louder, 0 - 100%. At 0% every note plays at full level.

X-MOD

A > B
Oscillator A modulates the phase of oscillator B (frequency modulation in the FM-synth manner), 0 - 100%. Adds bright, bell-like and metallic overtones to B. A is used as a modulator even if its LEVEL is at 0.
B > A
The other way round: B modulates A.

LFO 1 and LFO 2

WAVE
SINE, TRI, SQUARE or S&H (random steps).
RATE
LFO speed, 0.02 - 30 Hz.
SKEW
Shifts the shape. On SINE and TRI it moves the peak earlier or later; on SQUARE it sets the pulse width. On S&H, settings above 50% make the steps glide into each other.
DEST
What the LFO moves: OFF, PITCH (both paths), PITCH A, PITCH B, PAN (A one way, B the other), LEVEL (tremolo), X-MOD (the depth of both X-MOD knobs) or NOISE (the noise blend).
AMOUNT
How much it moves, 0 - 100%. On pitch the range grows towards the top of the knob, up to 12 semitones at 100%, so small settings give fine vibrato. The mod wheel adds to LFO 1's amount.
POLY / GLOBAL
POLY: every note has its own LFO, restarted when the note starts - each note gets the same vibrato or sweep. GLOBAL: one LFO for all notes, running freely. LFO 1 starts on POLY, LFO 2 on GLOBAL.

Dual delay

ON
Switches the delay in.
PING
Ping-pong: the sound goes into the left side only (summed to mono), and CROSS bounces it across to the right. Off: stereo in, stereo out.
SYNC L / SYNC R
The delay time of each side as a note value of the project tempo, from 1/32 to 1/1 (with triplet and dotted values), or OFF (ms) to set it in milliseconds.
TIME L / TIME R
The delay time in milliseconds, 1 - 2000 ms, used when that side's SYNC is OFF. The label is shown in brackets while the side is synced.
FEEDBACK
How much of each side repeats into itself, 0 - 95%.
CROSS
Cross-feedback: how much of each side repeats into the other, 0 - 95%. FEEDBACK and CROSS together are held below the point where the repeats would build up, so the delay always dies away.
LP / HP
Filters inside the feedback loop: LP (500 Hz - 20 kHz) darkens each repeat, HP (20 Hz - 2 kHz) thins it. The repeats get darker or thinner as they go.
MIX
How loud the delay is, 0 - 100%. The dry sound stays at full level.

Drift

A slow random wander, like old analogue tuning. DRIFT sets how much (0 - 100%, starting at 20%). In G Glow it moves the pitch of the whole instrument (up to 20 cents either way at 100%), lets oscillator B wander against A by up to 0.15 semitones, and gives each new note a slightly different tuning (up to 6 cents).

Master

DRIFT
See above.
VOLUME
Output level, -24 to +6 dB.
CHANNEL
The MIDI channel G Glow listens to: OMNI (all) or CH 1 to CH 16.
Meters
Output level, left and right.

IN / OUT strip

The slim strip along the bottom of the panel.

SAT
Band saturation on the way out: only the lows and highs are saturated (the mids stay clean), with a soft curve that loosens as the level rises.
OFF / CLIP / LIMIT
The final stage: nothing, a soft clipper (no latency), or a lookahead limiter that holds the peaks at -0.3 dBFS (3 ms latency, compensated by REAPER). CLIP is on by default; its key flashes red while it is clipping.
MONO
Makes everything below this frequency mono, from 20 to 500 Hz (fully left = OFF). Keeps the low end centred when paths are panned apart or the delay is wide.
WIDTH
Widens the stereo image above the MONO frequency, up to +100% (twice the side signal).

Presets

Each patch is levelled so that patches play at a similar loudness.

1981 Micro Chime
Both oscillators VINTAGE with 16 partials, B an octave up; fast-decaying chime, a touch of vibrato and a ping-pong delay.
Lo-Fi Bit Bass
MONO legato with a short glide; a VINTAGE bass made from the first four partials, a little LFSR noise and a sine an octave below on B.
Spectral Ambient Pad
MODERN with 64 partials, a formant set on A and a soft decay on B, detuned 7 cents either way; slow attack, slow LFO panning and a long delay.
E.Piano Tine
A bright tine on A with a fast decay over a warm sine body on B, a little A > B modulation and a gentle stereo pan.
Digital Sci-Fi Drone
Heavy A > B modulation with S&H on the X-MOD depth, slow panning and a dark, long-feedback delay.
8-Bit Arcade Percussion
A VINTAGE thump that drops in pitch (PITCH ENV) and an LFSR noise snap on B.
Dual-Timbral Poly
A saw on A hard left and a square on B hard right, slightly detuned, with a short delay.
8-Bit Arcade Snare
A short pitched thump on A and a bright LFSR noise body on B.
Glass Organ
VINTAGE drawbar-style partials on A, even partials an octave up on B, organ envelopes and a light vibrato; velocity off.
Vintage Strings
Two detuned VINTAGE saws with a slow swell, per-note vibrato and slow panning.
Hollow Flute
Odd partials an octave up with a little breath noise, per-note vibrato and a short delay.
Bell Tower
A spread, bell-like partial set on A and a high, detuned strike on B, long decays and a long delay.
Choir Formant
Two 64-partial formant sets, detuned, with slow swells, vibrato and slow panning.
Square Sync Lead
MONO legato with glide; a bright square on A, modulated by B (B > A), with vibrato and delay.
Init
A plain starting point: one MODERN saw on A, Path B silent, no LFOs, no delay.

Tips

  • Start from SHAPES, then draw on top: pull a few partials up or down and listen to how the tone changes.
  • For a classic 80s digital sound, set both oscillators to VINTAGE and use 16 partials; for a clean, hi-fi version of the same drawing, switch back to MODERN.
  • Give Path B a short envelope and a different partial set to add a separate attack or strike to a sustained sound.
  • Small amounts of A > B (5 - 15%) brighten a sound without making it obviously metallic.
  • If the delay or panned paths make the low end wander, raise MONO in the IN / OUT strip to 100 - 150 Hz.

File: gfx-g-glow.jsfx

Instruments

G Harbour Keys

A synthetic piano in the spirit of the early-90s ROM pianos, with a bell layer, tape wear, a mod rack and an 80s space.

Overview

G Harbour Keys is not a recreation of a real piano. It is a simulacrum of the piano sounds in the early-90s sample-based synths. Underneath is a physical model - hammers, strings, a soundboard and a bridge that lets the strings ring in sympathy - so it still answers your touch like an instrument.

On top of that model sit the things that made those ROM pianos sound the way they did: a glassy FM bell layer on every key, sample-rate and bit-depth reduction, a worn tape deck with wow and hiss, a mod rack (chorus, tremolo / auto-pan, vibe and ping-pong delay) and a room, plate or gated reverb.

Up to 16 keys can sound at once, across the 88 keys from A0 to C8. Twelve patches cover the classic territory, from bright house-style pianos to foggy, vaporwave tape keys.

Reach for it for

  • Glassy late-80s and early-90s pop and house piano parts
  • Vaporwave and lo-fi keys with tape wobble and a worn top end (WOW, VHS)
  • Electric-piano-like tines with tremolo pan (UPRIGHT, MALLET, BELL)
  • Gated-reverb ballads and big plate-drenched chords
  • Crunchy, aliasing sampler textures (ROM)

Playing it

G Harbour Keys is an instrument: it has no audio input and makes its sound from MIDI.

  1. Insert G Harbour Keys on a track (as the first plug-in, or after a MIDI effect).
  2. Arm the track for recording and set its input to your MIDI keyboard (or draw notes in a MIDI item).
  3. Play. The MIDI light at the top right flashes on each note; the PEDAL light shows when the sustain pedal is down.

It listens on all MIDI channels and plays notes from A0 to C8. It responds to the sustain pedal (CC64), the soft pedal (CC67, a softer, darker strike) and all-notes-off (CC120 and CC123). A note with velocity 1 - 3 lifts the key's dampers without striking it, so it can ring in sympathy with the other strings. The top keys (from F6 up) have no dampers, as on a real piano. Incoming MIDI is passed on down the chain.

Controls

The header

PRESETS, arrows and display
PRESETS (or a click on the display) opens the patch list; the ◄ / ► keys step to the previous or next patch. A patch sets every sound control; DRIFT, VOLUME, the stereo controls and the output stage are left as they are.
Display
Shows the patch number and name. A * after the number means you have changed a control since loading it. The second line shows GRAND or UPRT, the striker and PED:ON while the sustain pedal is down; while you turn a knob it shows that knob's name and value instead.
GRAND / UPRIGHT
The core model. GRAND has a deeper, longer-ringing soundboard and long sustain. UPRIGHT has shorter, stiffer bass strings, a smaller, higher soundboard, shorter sustain and a little more action knock. The panel changes colour with the model.
S / M / L
Panel size: small, medium or large. The panel also scales to fit the plugin window.
?
Opens a pop-up with the design credit, the GFX website address and the address of this manual. Click anywhere to close it.
MIDI / PEDAL
MIDI flashes on each note; PEDAL lights while the sustain pedal is held.

Engine

FELT / TACK / PLUCK / MALLET
What strikes the strings. FELT is a normal piano hammer. TACK puts metal on the wire: a shorter, brighter strike with a metallic click - the old tack-piano trick. PLUCK catches and releases the string like a plectrum, for a harpsichord-like edge. MALLET is heavy and soft, for a round, tine-like tone.
HARDNESS
The felt, 0 - 100%: soft and dark at the bottom, compacted and bright at the top. Harder felt gives a shorter, brighter attack.
DYNAMICS
How much your velocity changes the sound, 0 - 100%. Low settings even out your playing; high settings give a wide range from soft to loud.
SUSTAIN
How long notes ring while held, 0 - 100%. It also sets how long the bell layer rings. It reaches notes that are already sounding.
UNISON
Detunes the two or three strings of each key against each other, up to 28 cents at full - honky-tonk at the top of the range. The display shows the detune in cents while you turn it.
BELL
The level of the FM bell layer on every key: a fast tine transient over a sine body - the glassy top of the classic house piano and the FM electric piano.
GLASS
The brightness of the bell layer (its modulation index): low is a soft sine, high a glassy, metallic chime.

ROM & Tape

ROM
Sample-rate and bit-depth reduction, 0 - 100%: from 48 kHz / 16 bit down to about 10 kHz / 8 bit, with the aliasing left in. The sound of a cheap sample ROM.
WOW
Tape speed wander, 0 - 100%: a slow wow, a faster flutter and a little random drag. The vaporwave detune.
VHS
Worn tape, 0 - 100%: the top rolls off, hiss comes up and soft tape saturation squeezes the sound.

Mod rack

CHORUS: RATE / DEPTH / MIX
A stereo bucket-brigade-style chorus. RATE 0.05 - 8 Hz, DEPTH 0 - 100%, MIX 0 - 100% (0 = off).
TREM / PAN: RATE / DEPTH
Stereo auto-pan: the left and right sides dip in turn, the classic electric-piano trick. RATE 0.1 - 12 Hz, DEPTH 0 - 100% (0 = off).
VIBE: RATE / DEPTH
A four-stage photocell phaser, its lamp sweeping the notches. RATE 0.1 - 10 Hz, DEPTH 0 - 100% (0 = off).
DELAY: TIME / FEEDBACK / MIX
A ping-pong delay: the echoes start on the left and bounce across. The feedback is warm and saturating, losing top and bottom on each repeat. TIME 20 - 1500 ms, FEEDBACK 0 - 95%, MIX 0 - 100% (0 = off).
SYNC
Click to step through MS (TIME in milliseconds), 1/4, 1/8 D (dotted), 1/8 and 1/4 T (triplet). The note values follow the project tempo, up to 1.5 s; TIME is not used while synced.

Space

ROOM / PLATE / GATED
The reverb type. ROOM: a room of about 0.5 - 1.3 s (longer as SPACE goes up). PLATE: bright and long, about 1.4 - 4 s. GATED: the 80s gated reverb - a long, bright tail cut short about 0.3 s after each note.
SPACE
The amount of reverb, 0 - 100%. For ROOM and PLATE it also lengthens the tail.
SPREAD
Stereo spread across the keyboard, as heard from the player's seat: bass to the left, treble to the right. 0% is mono.
TONE
Overall brightness, 0 - 100 (50 = neutral): a tilt around 900 Hz, and the strings themselves lose their top more slowly as you turn it up.

MASTER

DRIFT
Gives every note its own small random tuning, up to 6 cents either way at 100% (15% by default), as on an instrument that has drifted slightly out of tune.
VOLUME
The output level, -24 to +6 dB.

OUTPUT

SAT
Band saturation on the way out, 0 - 100%: only the lows and highs are saturated (the mids stay clean), with a soft curve that loosens as the level rises.
OFF / CLIP / LIMIT
The final stage: nothing, a soft clipper at -0.3 dBFS (no latency), or a lookahead limiter (3 ms latency, compensated by REAPER). CLIP is on by default.

STEREO

MONO
Makes everything below this frequency mono (12 dB/oct), 20 - 500 Hz; fully left = OFF. Keeps the bass centred.
WIDTH
Raises the side signal above MONO, up to twice as wide at +100%.

In REAPER's parameter list only

The finer physical controls of the piano model have no knob on the panel, but they are in REAPER's parameter list (and every patch sets them).

Stiffness
How stiff the strings are: more stretches the upper partials sharper, for a harder, more metallic tone.
Damper
How quickly a note dies away when you let go of its key: higher settings let it ring on longer.
Body
How much of the soundboard's resonance you hear.
Sympathetic resonance
How strongly the strings share energy through the bridge: the other strings ring in sympathy, especially with the sustain pedal down.
Contact
How long the striker stays on the string: longer contact is softer and darker.
Bounce
Low settings let the action catch the hammer after the first blow; high settings let it bounce back for a second strike.
Wire age
Older strings: a duller top, shorter sustain and a little unevenness between the strings.
Tuning variance
Each key's own small tuning error (up to 6 cents), and a little spread between its strings.
Board size / Board density
Size moves the soundboard's resonances down (bigger) or up (smaller); a denser board rings longer and carries more of its upper resonances.
Lid
Open, Half or Closed: closing the lid makes the sound darker and boxier.
Mic distance
Close to far: further away is darker, quieter and narrower, with more room.
Bias / Sat low band / Sat high band / Ceiling
The rest of the output stage: the saturation's even-harmonic bias, where its low and high bands start (40 - 400 Hz and 2 - 16 kHz) and the clip ceiling or limiter threshold.

Presets

The twelve factory patches. Choose them from PRESETS or the display, or step through them with the ◄ / ► keys.

Harbour Lights
Bright grand with tack strikers, a bell layer, a touch of ROM, chorus and a dotted-eighth delay on the plate.
Ocean Drive EP
Upright with mallets and a strong bell: an electric-piano tone with tremolo pan, chorus and a quarter-note delay.
Neon Rain
Felt grand through ROM, tape wow and VHS wear, with deep chorus and a dotted-eighth delay.
Sunset Strip
Hard, bright tack piano with a bell layer, a little ROM grit, chorus and an eighth-note delay in a small room.
Mall Fountain
Soft, long-sustaining grand through heavy wow and VHS, with vibe, chorus, a 500 ms delay and lots of plate.
Night Bus
Dark upright with mallets and the lid closed, worn tape, slow tremolo pan and a room.
Palm Glass
Grand with tacks and a loud, very glassy bell layer, chorus and delay on the plate.
Cassette Ballad
Felt grand, half lid, long sustain, gentle tape wow and VHS, chorus and plate.
Gated Romance
Bright tack grand into the 80s gated reverb, with bell and chorus.
Pastel Tine
Upright with mallets and a bright bell: a tine-like tone through vibe, light chorus and tape.
Floppy Disk
Upright tack piano crushed by heavy ROM reduction, in a small room.
Harbour Fog
Very soft, long-sustaining grand with mallets, half lid, tape wear, chorus, vibe and a long 600 ms delay in a big plate.

Tips

  • Start from a patch and change the striker: the same patch with FELT, TACK or MALLET gives three quite different instruments.
  • For the classic house-piano top, raise BELL and GLASS together; for a softer electric-piano feel, use UPRIGHT, MALLET and some TREM / PAN.
  • ROM, WOW and VHS add up quickly: a little of each often sounds more convincing than a lot of one.
  • Hold the sustain pedal and play: with Sympathetic resonance up, the undamped strings ring along with what you play.
  • If the low end gets wide and woolly in a mix, set MONO to about 120 - 150 Hz.

File: gfx-g-harbour-keys.jsfx

Instruments

G Lab80

A late-80s digital sound workstation: pick one of 43 sounds and play it.

Overview

G Lab80 is a sound-module instrument in the spirit of the late-80s digital workstations. There are no oscillator pages to program: you pick a sound from the list, shape it with eight macro knobs and play. The sounds cover pianos and keys, mallets and bells, plucked guitars, pads, choirs, strings, brass and leads, and basses.

Under the hood each sound uses the engine that suits it: four-operator FM for the e-pianos, a plucked string for the clav, harp and guitars, struck inharmonic partials for the glockenspiel, an openly synthetic struck-string model for the pianos, a tonewheel-style partial bank for the organ, and detuned saws and pulses through a 4-pole filter for the analogue sounds. Some sounds add bell partials, breath, a chiff, vocal formants, their own tempo-synced echo, tremolo or a fast rotary-style chorus.

A REVERB section (ROOM, HALL or PLATE) is added on top as a send, so the dry sound always stays at full level. Up to 12 notes play at once; the bass and lead sounds marked mono below play one note at a time and glide between them.

Reach for it for

  • Quick 80s and 90s keys, pads and bells without programming a synth
  • Plucked guitar and harp layers in the late-80s style
  • Choirs, strings and orchestra hits for pop and soundtrack work
  • Mono basses with glide, from sine sub to acid and reese
  • Sketching ideas fast: step through the sounds with < and >

Playing it

G Lab80 is an instrument: it has no audio input and makes its sound from MIDI.

  1. Insert G Lab80 on a track (as the first plug-in, or after a MIDI effect).
  2. Arm the track for recording and set its input to your MIDI keyboard (or draw notes in a MIDI item).
  3. Pick a sound with ◄ and ►, or click PRESETS (or the display) for the list grouped by type.
  4. Play. The MIDI IN light flashes on each note, and the display shows how many notes are sounding.
  5. If you use a multi-channel MIDI setup, set CHANNEL in the MIDI section to the channel you want; OMNI listens to all of them.

G Lab80 responds to the sustain pedal (CC64), the mod wheel (CC1, vibrato on the analogue sounds and the FM e-pianos) and all-notes-off (CC120 and CC123). Incoming MIDI is passed on down the chain. Sounds with their own echo or with the NATIVE DANCE drum loop follow the project tempo; when the transport is stopped they run on at that tempo.

Controls

The header

S / M / L
Panel size: small, medium or large. The panel also scales to fit the plugin window.
?
Opens a pop-up with the design credit, the GFX website address and the address of this manual. Click anywhere to close it.
PANIC
Lets go of every held note at once (and of the sustain pedal), so stuck notes die away.

Sound

PRESETS and the ◄ ► keys sit side by side to the left of the display.

PRESETS
Opens the sound list, grouped into sub-menus by type.
◄ and ►
Step to the previous or next sound, in the order of the list (by type).
Display
Shows the sound's name, its group and its place in the group (e.g. PADS 2/4), how many notes are sounding, and SUS while the sustain pedal is down. Click it to open the sound list too.

Changing the sound stops any notes still sounding and puts FILTER, RESO, TONE, ATTACK, RELEASE and CHORUS back to neutral, so each sound starts from its own settings.

MACROS

Eight knobs that bend the chosen sound. At their neutral settings you hear the sound as it was designed.

FILTER
Moves the filter, from -6 to +3 octaves; at 0 it reads OPEN. On the analogue sounds (pads, strings, brass, leads, basses, choirs, plucks) it moves each note's own 4-pole filter up or down. On the pianos, FM e-pianos, clav, glockenspiel and organ it is a resonant low-pass on the output: 0 is wide open, and turning it down darkens the sound.
RESO
Filter resonance, 0 - 100%. On the analogue sounds it adds to the sound's own resonance, up to self-oscillation at the top. On the other sounds it adds a resonant peak to the output low-pass.
TONE
Brightness, 0 - 100% (50% is neutral). On the analogue sounds it moves the filter up to 1.5 octaves either way; on the FM e-pianos it sets how much bark the tine has; on the plucked strings it brightens or darkens the string; on the pianos it makes the hammers harder or softer.
ATTACK
Scales the attack of the analogue sounds' envelopes, 10 - 400% (100% is the sound's own). Lower is snappier, higher swells in more slowly. It never goes faster than 3 ms, so nothing clicks.
RELEASE
Scales how long most sounds ring on after you let go, 10 - 400% (100% is the sound's own).
CHORUS
Scales the sound's own chorus (or ensemble), 0 - 200%. Sounds without chorus, such as most basses, are not changed by it.
VELOCITY
How much playing harder makes notes louder, 0 - 100% (70% by default). At 0% every note plays at the same level. The struck and plucked sounds still get brighter when you play harder.
TUNE
Fine tuning, -50 to +50 cents.

REVERB

A send reverb added on top of the sound: the dry sound stays at full level and MIX sets how much reverb joins it.

ROOM / HALL / PLATE
The space: a small room, a large hall, or a dense, bright plate.
PRE-DELAY
The gap before the reverb starts, 0 - 150 ms (15 ms by default).
DECAY
How long the reverb lasts, 0.2 - 10 s (2.2 s by default).
DAMPING
How quickly the top end of the tail dies away, 0 - 100%. Higher is darker.
MIX
How loud the reverb is, 0 - 100% (25% by default). At 0% the reverb is off.

Drift

DRIFT
How much the instrument wanders, 0 - 100% (25% by default). The pitch drifts slowly (up to 20 cents at 100%) on the analogue, FM and plucked sounds; each analogue note starts slightly out of tune (up to 6 cents) and slightly brighter or darker; the filter wanders; and the chorus speed drifts a little. At 0% everything stays exact.

MIDI

CHANNEL
The MIDI channel G Lab80 listens to: OMNI (all) or CH 1 to CH 16.
MIDI IN
Lights when a note arrives or notes are sounding.

MASTER

OUTPUT
Output level, -24 to +12 dB.
OUT meters
Output level, left and right, with a floating peak. Click a meter to clear the peaks.

The output is softly clipped at -0.3 dBFS, so peaks never go over.

Sounds

The sound list, in the order of the menu. Sounds marked mono play one note at a time and glide from the last note.

Pianos & Keys

Session Piano
A warm, even, openly synthetic piano: struck partials with stretched tuning and paired strings. Playing harder brightens the hammer, adds knock and makes the top fade faster.
Bright Piano
The same piano with harder hammers, more knock and a wider shimmer between the strings.
Chorus EP
The FM tine e-piano with a stereo tremolo and a deep chorus.
FM E-Piano
The four-operator FM tine piano; velocity sets how hard the tine barks.
Clav
A bright plucked string heard from a pickup near its end, with bite and a quick damper when you let go.
Drawbar Organ
An organ from sine partials with third-harmonic percussion on fresh chords, a key click and a fast rotary-style chorus.

Mallets & Bells

Glockenspiel
A struck metal bar: inharmonic partials, a soft mallet click, and it rings on.
Fantasy Bells
Bell partials over a slow, detuned saw pad with chorus.
Crystal Harp
Chiming, slightly detuned partials over a soft, bright pad with a deep chorus.
Staccato Glass
Short 'aah' voices with glassy bell partials and an echo.

Guitars & Plucks

LA Nylon
A round pluck with a soft pick over a quiet sine body that swells in.
LA Steel
A bright steel-string pick with a shimmering synth body.
LA Clean
A chorused electric guitar (pickup near the bridge), with echo.
LA Harmonics
Glassy string harmonics over a soft pluck, with echo.
Nylon Atmos
A plucked string over a slow, warm pad, with echo.
Pizz Flow
A bright, short pluck with bell partials and a quick echo.
Harp
A long, round pluck.

Pads

Synth Pad
Detuned saws through a slowly opening 4-pole filter, with chorus.
Warm Pad
Saw and sub under a low filter that moves slowly, with a wide chorus.
Soundtrack Pad
A chime over a slow, warm filter sweep, with echo.
Native Dance
A tempo-synced log-drum loop, retuned to each held note, riding a dark resonant sweep, with echo.

Choirs & Voices

Glass Voices
A slow 'aah' choir with breath and vibrato.
Dark Vox
A low, slow 'ooh' choir.
Fantasy Vox
A bright 'aah' with a glassy chime on the front.
Spaced Vox
Airy 'eh' voices with a slowly drifting filter and a long echo.

Strings & Orchestra

Strings
Detuned saws with a slow attack and delayed vibrato.
String Ensemble
Bright saws into a three-phase ensemble.
Fat Strings
Thick saws with a little sub into the ensemble.
Orchestra Hit
A big octave stab that drops in pitch, with a burst of noise on the front.

Brass & Leads

Brass Section
A swelling filter, a small rise into each note and delayed vibrato; playing harder opens it up.
Synth Brass
Punchy saw brass with a strong filter envelope.
Velo Stab
A short stab: playing harder opens the filter right up.
Hoover
Mono. Wide, detuned saws and pulses that scoop up into the note, with a heavy chorus.
Square Lead
Mono. Two slightly detuned squares with glide, vibrato and echo.
Calliope Lead
A pipe: a sine with a little square, pitched breath, a short chiff, vibrato and echo.
Pan Flute
A sine with its breath tuned to the note, a soft chiff and vibrato.

Basses

Bass Synth
Mono. Saw, square and sub with a punchy filter envelope.
Sub Bass
Mono. A pure sine.
Acid Bass
Mono. A resonant saw with a snappy filter envelope and slides.
Round Bass
Mono. Saw, square and sub.
Pluck Bass
Short and punchy, and polyphonic.
Hollow Bass
Mono. Sine and square.
Reese Bass
Mono. Wide, detuned saws with a little sub.

Tips

  • Use RELEASE and ATTACK to turn a sound into something else: a short RELEASE makes a pad into a stab, a long ATTACK makes a pluck swell in.
  • On the analogue sounds, a little RESO with FILTER turned down gives the classic squelchy sweep; automate FILTER for builds.
  • Set VELOCITY to 0% for even, sequenced parts; raise it for played keys and pianos.
  • The basses and leads that glide are mono: play legato (overlapping notes) to hear the glide.
  • If the sound is too wet in a busy mix, pull the REVERB MIX down and use a send reverb shared with the rest of the track list.

File: gfx-g-lab80.jsfx

Instruments

G Lead 8

An eight-voice analogue-style polysynth for leads and poly parts, with a built-in arpeggiator.

Overview

G Lead 8 is a classic subtractive synth: two oscillators per voice into a warm 24 dB ladder filter, with its own envelopes for the filter and the amp, and an LFO for vibrato, pulse-width and filter movement. It plays up to eight notes at once, or one at a time in MONO for legato leads with glide.

A built-in arpeggiator turns held chords into tempo-locked patterns, and twelve patches give you starting points from classic leads to brass, strings and wobble.

GFX drift keeps it alive: the tuning wanders slowly, each voice's oscillators drift apart a little on their own, each voice's pulse width wanders, and every note lands slightly differently in tune. The voices are spread a little across the stereo field.

Reach for it for

  • Classic mono synth leads with glide
  • Poly brass, strings and soft pads
  • Arpeggiated parts locked to the project
  • PWM and detuned textures
  • Resonant filter sweeps and wobbles

Playing it

  1. Insert G Lead 8 on a track as an instrument (it has no audio input; it plays from MIDI).
  2. Arm the track for recording with a MIDI input and turn on record monitoring, or draw notes in a MIDI item.
  3. The MIDI light in the header blinks green when notes arrive. If you use more than one MIDI channel, set CHANNEL to the one you want; OMNI plays on all.
  4. Pitch bend covers +/-2 semitones, the mod wheel (CC1) adds vibrato, the sustain pedal (CC64) holds notes, and CC123 / CC120 stop all notes.

Controls

The header

Patch display
Shows the patch number and name, POLY or MONO, how many of the eight voices are sounding, and ARP ON when the arpeggiator is running. Click it for the list of patches.
< / >
Step to the previous or next patch.
MIDI
Blinks green when a note arrives.
S / M / L
Panel size: small, medium or large. The panel also scales to fit the plugin window.
?
Opens a pop-up with the design credit, the GFX website address and the address of this manual. Click anywhere to close it.

OSCILLATORS

SAW / PULSE (OSC 1, OSC 2)
The waveform of each oscillator. SAW is bright and buzzy; PULSE is hollower and changes with PW.
COARSE
Oscillator 2's pitch in semitones, -24 to +24. +7 gives fifths, +12 or -12 an octave.
FINE
Oscillator 2's fine tuning, -50 to +50 cents. A few cents of detune against oscillator 1 gives the thick, beating analogue sound.
MIX
The balance between the two oscillators, from OSC 1 only to OSC 2 only.
PW
Pulse width for the PULSE waves, 5 to 95%. 50% is a square; narrower settings are thinner and more nasal.

FILTER

CUTOFF
The ladder filter's cutoff frequency, 20 Hz to 18 kHz. Lower is darker.
RESONANCE
0 to 100%. Emphasises the frequencies around the cutoff, from smooth to squelchy; near the top it starts to ring.
ENV
How far the filter envelope moves the cutoff, -100% to +100% (up to six octaves). Negative values close the filter instead of opening it.
KEY
Key tracking, 0 to 100%. At 100% the cutoff follows the keyboard, so high notes are as bright as low ones.

FILTER ENVELOPE and AMP ENVELOPE

Both envelopes have the same four controls. The filter envelope moves the cutoff (by the ENV amount); the amp envelope shapes the volume of each note.

ATTACK
How fast it rises when a key goes down, 0.5 ms to 3 s.
DECAY
How fast it falls to the sustain level, 5 ms to 5 s.
SUSTAIN
The level held while the key is down, 0 to 100%.
RELEASE
How fast it dies away after the key is released, 5 ms to 5 s.

LFO

SHAPE
SINE, TRI, SAW, SQUARE or S&H (sample and hold: a new random value each cycle).
RATE
The LFO speed, 0.05 to 20 Hz.
VIBRATO
How much the LFO moves the pitch, 0 to 100 cents. The mod wheel adds up to another 50 cents on top.
PWM
How much the LFO moves the pulse width, 0 to 100%. Only heard on PULSE waves.
CUTOFF
How much the LFO moves the filter cutoff, 0 to 100% (up to three octaves each way) - for wah and wobble.

ARPEGGIATOR

The GFX arpeggiator, built in. With ARP on, held notes play as an arpeggio, every step on. It locks to the project tempo when the project plays and runs free at the project tempo from the first key when it is stopped.

ARP
Switches the arpeggiator on (red light) or off. Switching it stops any notes it was playing.
LATCH
On: the arpeggio keeps playing after you let go. Release all keys and play a new chord to replace it.
PANIC
Stops every note at once.
MODE
UP, DOWN, UP-DN, DN-UP, PLAYED (the order you played the notes), RANDOM or CHORD (the whole chord on every step).
RATE
The step length: 1/4, 1/8, 1/8T, 1/16, 1/16T or 1/32 (T = triplet).
OCTAVES
1 to 4: how many octaves upwards the arpeggio repeats the notes.
GATE
How long each note is held, 10 to 200% of its step. Above 100% the notes overlap.
SWING
0 to 75%: lengthens the first step of each pair and shortens the second.

PERFORMANCE

POLY / MONO
POLY plays up to eight notes. MONO plays one note at a time, legato: a new note held over an old one glides to it without a new attack, the last note wins, and letting go returns to a note still held. Switching stops all notes.
GLIDE
Portamento time, 0 to 1000 ms. In MONO the pitch slides from note to note; in POLY each voice slides from the last note it played.
VELOCITY
0 to 100%: how much playing harder makes notes louder and opens the filter envelope further. 0 ignores velocity.
DRIFT
0 to 100% (25% by default). At 100% the whole instrument's tuning wanders by up to 20 cents, each voice's oscillators drift apart by up to 8 cents and its pulse width wanders, and each note is struck up to 6 cents out of tune. 0 turns it all off.
VOLUME
Output level, -24 to +6 dB.
CHANNEL
The MIDI channel it listens to: OMNI (all) or 1 to 16.

Patches

Choosing a patch sets the oscillators, filter, both envelopes, the LFO, GLIDE and POLY / MONO; the arpeggiator, VELOCITY, DRIFT, VOLUME and CHANNEL are left as they are. Every patch is level-matched so a single-note line plays at about the same loudness.

Classic Lead
Two saws a few cents apart, a moderate filter sweep and a little vibrato; mono.
Poly Brass
Detuned saws with a slow-opening filter and soft attack: synth brass; poly.
Fat Mono
Saws an octave apart with more resonance and a 60 ms glide; mono.
Square Lead
Two square waves an octave apart, bright, with sine vibrato and glide; mono.
PWM Strings
Detuned pulses with a slow LFO on the pulse width, slow attack and long release; poly.
Hollow Pipe
Narrow pulses an octave apart with resonance and vibrato: a reedy pipe; mono.
Fifths
Two saws a fifth apart with glide; mono.
Soft Poly
Saw and pulse through a darker filter with a falling amp envelope; poly.
Resonant Sweep
A low, highly resonant filter swept hard by its envelope; mono.
Vintage Detune
Saws 18 cents apart with gentle vibrato and a longer release; poly.
Wobble Lead
Saws an octave apart with a 4 Hz LFO on the cutoff; mono.
Init
A plain starting point: two slightly detuned saws; poly.

Tips

  • For a classic lead, use MONO with a little GLIDE and leave VIBRATO at zero - then add vibrato with the mod wheel as you play.
  • For slowly opening swells, raise the filter envelope's ATTACK and set ENV well above zero with CUTOFF low.
  • The output stage (SAT and OFF / CLIP / LIMIT, CLIP by default) and the stereo controls (mono below a frequency, width) have no knobs on the panel; you can reach them in REAPER's parameter list.
  • With the arpeggiator on, try CHORD mode with a short GATE for rhythmic stabs.

File: gfx-g-lead-8.jsfx

Instruments

G M1K

A six-voice analogue rack synth with 100 patches, a full editor and a built-in effects rack.

Overview

G M1K is a six-voice analogue synth module in the spirit of the late-80s matrix-modulation rack synths: a single rack unit full of patches, with a full editor behind the EDIT key. Each voice has two DCOs, a 24 dB low-pass VCF, two envelopes and two LFOs.

It comes with 100 factory patches in seven banks - KEYBOARD, STRINGS, WIND+PERC, SYNTH, BASS, LEAD and EFFECTS. Every patch also carries its own Mod Rack setting: chorus, tremolo, vibe and delay built into the unit.

It is an instrument: it has no audio input and plays from MIDI. It responds to pitch bend, the mod wheel (CC1), channel aftertouch, the sustain pedal (CC64) and all-notes-off (CC120 / CC123).

Reach for it for

  • Classic late-80s analogue pads, strings and brass
  • Punchy synth basses and mono leads with glide
  • Quick patch browsing - 100 sounds a click away
  • Building your own patches in the editor
  • Wide stereo sounds with the built-in Mod Rack

Loading and playing it

  1. Insert G M1K on a track (it is an instrument, so it needs no audio on the track).
  2. Arm the track for recording and set its input to your MIDI keyboard (or draw MIDI notes into an item on the track).
  3. Click the patch name window and choose a patch, or step through them with the < and > keys. The MIDI light flashes when notes arrive.
  4. If you play on a particular MIDI channel, set CH in the PLAY strip; OMNI listens to all 16.

Controls

The rack unit

Patch number
The red three-digit display shows the patch number, 000 to 099.
Patch name window
Shows the bank and the patch name. While you turn a control it shows that control's name and value for a moment instead. Click it for every patch, listed by bank.
< / >
Step to the previous or next patch (wrapping round from 099 to 000).
EDIT
Switches the lower half of the panel between the patch list and the editor.
BLACK / CREAM
Swaps the faceplate between the black early boxes and the cream 90s boxes. Looks only - the sound does not change.
MIDI
Lights when a note arrives.
VOLUME
The overall level, -24 to +6 dB.
